## 1. What this is, and what its length does not mean

Rule R-e requires every converted path to preserve its error-to-response
mapping - same status codes, same error payload shapes. The migration
preserves three shared funnels verbatim, and that is **necessary but not
sufficient**: the funnels are reached from hundreds of local branches, each
branch decides *which* funnel its error reaches, and some reach none. This
document is one checkbox row per changed error edge, so the claim "the error
mappings survived" can be inspected rather than asserted.

**The row count is not a target and is not authoritative.** This run emitted 332 rows over 12 files. A different tree
yields a different number, and that is the intended behaviour: conversion
closes rows.

**245 of the 342 baseline edges are closed** against this tree, and **96** are not.
A row is closed when the edge produces the same observable outcome on both
trees - same funnel, same served status, same answers-or-does-not - so a
ticked box is a comparison and not an opinion. Section 8 lists every open
row with both outcomes side by side, and section 9 says which rows a
measured request actually reached, because a closed row is a reading of two
trees and not a response anyone observed.

### What is in scope, and what deliberately is not

The analysed surface is the 12 files hapi itself invokes into: the ten
controllers, the named pre-handlers in `lib/util/helpers.js`, and the one
inline pre-handler in `config/api_routes.js`. Every one of them is read,
and a file with no row says so in its own section rather than being omitted.

Nothing else is reviewed. Rule R-a requires the diff to read as a runtime bump,
a hapi API migration, an async conversion and blocking-only dependency swaps -
so this is a record of the error edges **that conversion moves**, not a general
audit of the application's error handling. The internal callback modules keep
their callback interfaces under rule T-3, which puts the `await` boundary at the
lifecycle method rather than pushing it into the utility, so their internal
error edges are not conversion sites and carry no row here. Where a converted
handler awaits one of them, the edge that appears below is the handler's own.

### The sizing metric is not the row count

AAP 0.1.1.1 records **132 functions with an `err` parameter** in `lib/controllers/**`. That figure is a
**sizing metric and nothing else.** A crude grep over the baseline tree returns
133 rather than 132 depending on the pattern, which is itself the point:

- one callback can carry **zero** error dispositions - it never inspects `err` -
  or **several**: a guard, a log, and a late resolve;
- promise `.catch` handlers and synchronous `throw` statements add edges
  **entirely outside** that count;
- a `reply(` site is an error edge only when what it carries is an error, and at
  baseline 61 of the 202 sites carry success payloads.

This run counted 132 `err`-parameter functions in the ten controllers and
emitted 332 rows. Neither number derives from the other. Do not read the row
count as a coverage figure for the sizing metric, or the sizing metric as a
target for the row count.

## 2. The three shared funnels

Every row names the funnel its edge reaches. Line ranges below are read from
the analysed tree, not quoted from the plan, so they are correct for whichever
tree this document describes.

### Layer 1 - the handler catch-all, `lib/util/routeParser.js:468-479`

Logs `err.stack` when present, else `String(err)`, and returns
`Boom.badImplementation(err.message || String(err))` - so **500**. After the
async conversion this becomes structural: a rejecting `async` handler lands
here without anything having to call it.

Two properties of this block decide many rows below.

1. **The `if (err)` guard at `:469` is preserved.** A falsy throw satisfies the `catch` but
   fails the guard, so the block produces **no return**, and hapi's toolkit
   converts that `undefined` into the same status with a different message.
2. **It does not test `isBoom`.** A `throw Boom.notFound()` inside a
   handler is therefore answered **500** with Boom's hidden 5xx body, not 404,
   while `return Boom.notFound()` is answered 404. Same value, different verb,
   different status. Rows distinguish the two.

### Layer 2 - `request.fail`, `lib/util/routeParser.js:376-405`

Logs at info level, then selects one of three responses:

- negotiated `html` **and** a configured `fail.redirect`: flashes `failure` (`:384`),
  **interpolates `fail.redirect` in place** (`:385`) - the cross-request state leak,
  because the object being mutated was captured once at parse time - flashes
  `payload` and `query`, and redirects;
- negotiated `html`, a configured `fail.html` and no `.json` extension on the
  path: renders that view after `addUserContext`;
- otherwise: defaults the payload to `{}`, attaches `json.flash`, and returns
  `h.response(json)`.

None of those three is a Boom, so a Layer 2 response takes Layer 3's non-Boom
branch and **does** receive the cache headers.

The in-place interpolation is the **cross-request state leak**: the `fail`
object was captured once at parse time and the handler closure holds it by
reference, so the assignment mutates the long-lived object and a later request
to the same route redirects to the earlier request's target. It is catalogued,
with its measurement and its blast radius, in `docs/preserved-quirks.md` section 3.
R-d requires it: rows on this funnel preserve the mutation rather than
repairing it.

### Layer 3 - `onPreResponse`, `app.js:177-226`

For a Boom response it computes `isApiRequest` at `:186` from three tests: a path
beginning `/api/`, an `Accept` containing `application/json`, or a path
beginning `/partials/`.

**Branch order matters, and the code's shape misleads on it.** For a browser
HTML request the extension **returns immediately** on 401 (`h.redirect('/login')`
with `.takeover()` at `:197`), on 404, on 403 and on any status >= 500 -
**before** the header assignments that begin at `:207`. So:

- `Cache-Control`, `Pragma`, `Expires` and the conditional `X-Frame-Options`
  reach **API/JSON error responses and non-Boom responses only** (the non-Boom branch re-applies them at `:216`);
- they do **not** reach the rendered HTML error pages;
- a Boom status outside that set - a **400**, for instance - falls through the
  early returns and **does** receive them.

This is a correction R-e forced on an earlier reading of the code's shape. It
is stated here so nobody re-derives it wrongly from the source order.

`X-Frame-Options: deny` applies only to the 5 paths at `config/default.yaml:353-358`: `/`, `/login`, `/signup`, `/contact`, `/educators`.

### Not a funnel, but decisive: how a pre-handler error is answered, `lib/util/routeParser.js:102-193`

Pre-handlers are not invoked by the handler wrapper, so **their errors never
reach Layer 1**. What answers them instead depends on whether the analysed
tree still carries the response emulation, and this tree **does not**.

`convertPreHandlers` passes a function-valued pre-handler to hapi as it is,
so hapi's own contract applies. Read from the installed framework rather
than remembered:

- a returned or thrown **Boom** keeps its own status. `Response.wrap` returns
  it unchanged, `isBoom` routes it to the route's pre `failAction`, and the
  default `'error'` throws it - so **Layer 3** answers with that status
  (`@hapi/hapi/lib/handler.js`, the `if (response.isBoom)` branch of the pre
  path).
- a returned **plain `Error`** is **not** resolved as the pre value. It is
  boomified - `Response.wrap` calls `Boom.boomify` on any `Error` - and then
  takes the same path, so the request is answered **500** through Layer 3.
  **This is where the two contracts differ, and it is the reason a
  pre-handler that used to hand an `Error` on as its assigned value must
  now return a non-`Error` value to keep the request continuing.**
- returning **`undefined`** - falling off the end of the function - becomes
  `Boom.badImplementation`, so a pre-handler with nothing to contribute must
  `return null`, which is a pre value of `null` and not an error.

The string form is not a lifecycle method at all: the dispatcher wraps it in
its own `async (request, h)` and returns `serverMethod.apply(null, args)`, so
a server method's throw rejects that wrapper and reaches Layer 3 the same
way. Rows on the `server method` surface say so.

## 3. Two dispositions a mechanical conversion changes silently

These two are called out because converting them correctly looks like
converting them wrongly. Both appear as row dispositions below.

### log-and-continue

The handler logs and **returns nothing**, so the surrounding flow continues and
this edge produces no response. Mechanically converting such a handler into a
rejection - or into a returned Boom - turns an edge that produces no response
into one that does, and changes what a client receives from whatever else
settles the request into a 500.

The clearest instance is the streaming asset fetch, whose `error` listener only
`console.log`s. Two measured details belong with it, because they are different
failure modes with different outcomes:

- a **refused connection** emits `error` and **not** `end`, so the upload
  continuation never runs, **nothing is uploaded at all**, and the request is
  left **unsettled**. An earlier reading of this site had it uploading partial
  bytes; that was wrong, and the distinction matters because the two failure
  modes need different fixtures and produce different observable results;
- a **mid-stream failure after `response`** may still reach `end`, so *there*
  the continuation does run, on a partial file.

Both are baseline behaviour and both are preserved.

### resolve-on-later-callback

The response is produced inside a continuation-passing callback, so it settles
only when that operation calls back, and what a client receives today is
whichever settlement wins. These must **not** be collapsed into an earlier
`await`: rule T-3 puts the await boundary at the call site, and moving the
settlement earlier can change the response itself. Where such a callback also
declares no error parameter, the operation's own failure is dropped, and the row
says so.

## 4. How to read a row

```
  ... - [ ] `<file>:<line>` - <Disposition> - <Funnel> - <Shape>
  ...       Carrier <reachable code path> - Routes <bound routes> - Source <the line>
  ...       Target: <the outcome to preserve, ending in Side effects and Timing>
  ...       Note: <a measured detail, when there is one>
  ...       See also: <the sibling document that catalogues this edge>
```

A real row is a line beginning `- [ ] ` or `- [x] ` at column 0, and its
continuation lines are indented six spaces and begin `Carrier `, `Target: `,
`Closure: `, `Driven: `, `Note: ` or `See also: `. Every line of the sketch
above carries a `... ` prefix that a real row does not, so counting lines
matching `^- \[[ x]\] ` returns the row count this document states and
nothing else - the sketch does not inflate it by one.

### The join contract

**Every row carries a stable id**, printed in the row as `` `id=...` `` and
shaped `<file>.<carrier>.<class>.<ordinal>`. It deliberately contains no
line number, no disposition and no funnel, because conversion changes all
three: a `reply(err)` site becomes `return errors.notFound()` and moves from
`calls reply(err)` to `returns or throws a Boom`, so an id
carrying either would never match its own target row - and matching a
baseline row to its target row is the one comparison the id exists to make.

That id is the join key in both directions:

| Consumer | How it joins |
|---|---|
| a reviewer | `grep 'id=<the id>'` in this file |
| `test/parity/capture.js` | put the id in a scenario's **`coversEdges`** array - NOT in `covers`; this generator then reports that row as **edge-level** driven |
| `test/parity/replay.js`, or any tool | read the machine-readable index this generator writes with `--edge-index <path>`: schema `trinket-oss/error-edge-index@2`, holding one record per TARGET row and one per BASELINE row, each with the id, file, carrier, surface, class, disposition, funnel, served status, the dimensions that differ, the closure verdict and the driver - plus an `unpaired` block naming every row missing from the target, new in the target, ambiguous, or proven unreachable |

**`coversEdges`, and not `covers`.** `test/parity/capture.js` validates every
`covers` entry against the route manifest and reports anything that is not a
declared route as `unknownRoutes`, which it treats as a defect in its own
tables. So a scenario naming an edge id in `covers` fails the producer, and
an earlier edition of this document told authors to put them there - a join
contract the producer cannot satisfy. `coversEdges` is a field capture.js
does not read, so a scenario carries it without tripping that validation,
and it is the field this generator names. The change that closes the join is
one line per scenario in the `error-edge.*` groups of
`test/parity/capture.js` - `coversEdges: ['<edge id>']` alongside the
existing route-keyed `covers` - and that file belongs to the
capture-scenario unit, not to this generator. Until it lands, edge-level
coverage is 0 and section 9 reports route-level coverage instead,
which is weaker: a scenario that reaches a route does not establish that it
reached a particular error branch within it.

So this document is no longer the only carrier of its own contents, and a
consumer never has to parse the prose. The previous edition of this file
said "Nothing machine-reads this document", and that was the problem rather
than a design note: with 341 rows and no key, the corpus could not state
which edge a scenario reached, and no tool could report that a changed edge
had never been driven.

**Disposition** is a closed vocabulary and every row carries exactly one
value from it. Six of the values are the ones AAP 0.6.3 enumerates:

- `calls request.fail locally`
- `calls reply(err)`
- `returns or throws a Boom`
- `logs and continues`
- `swallows silently`
- `resolves on a later callback`

The seventh is an addition this generator makes, and it is called out rather
than slipped in:

- `propagates to its caller`

It exists because none of the six describes a callback that hands its error
to an outer continuation - `reject(err)`, `resolve({err : err, ...})`,
`next(err)` - and R-e's deliverable is worth having only if its rows are
true. Filing such an edge under `swallows silently` states two
false things at once: that the error is absorbed, and that no response can
follow from it. Filing a callback that logs *and* rejects under
`logs and continues` states a third, that the normal path
continues, when the continuation is a rejection. The value takes precedence
over both, and each such row names the vehicle and the line so the hop can
be checked against the source.

**Shape** carries the sub-shape that six values cannot express - `reply(err)`
with no return, a bare `.catch(request.fail)` reference, a synchronous throw, a
CPS callback boundary. Shape is descriptive and open; Disposition is closed.

**Funnel** is `Layer 1`, `Layer 2`, `Layer 3` or `none`. `none` is a legitimate and
important value: an edge that produces no response at all is the edge most
likely to be given one by accident.

**Target** states the outcome to **preserve** - status, payload or redirect,
side effects, timing. **No row proposes a fix.** R-d prohibits improvements, so
where the baseline outcome is a defect the row requires the defect.

**Carrier** is the reachable code path - the exported handler or pre-handler
the edge sits in - and **Routes** are the literal route declarations that bind
it. Every row carries a carrier, so every row is drivable; see section 11.

**See also** appears on a row whose edge is also catalogued elsewhere, and is
the only form of duplication this document permits.

### What this document does not own

Three documents describe the same edges from different angles, and each claim
belongs to exactly one of them:

| Document | Owns |
|---|---|
| this document | the per-edge status, payload or redirect, side effects and timing, and the funnel each edge must still reach |
| `docs/preserved-quirks.md` | why a baseline defect is kept, its measurement, and the two approved deviations |
| `docs/conversion-inventory.md` | the per-site return/await disposition for every handler body the conversion touches |

So a row states the outcome to preserve and points at the quirk catalogue for
the reasoning; it does not restate it. Every row whose Target carries the
return-discipline sentence is also a conversion site, and the call sites where
a missing `return` is what makes the conversion decisive carry an explicit
`See also` to `docs/conversion-inventory.md`.

**Those are CALL-SITE counts, and they are not error-edge counts.** The
distinction is not pedantry: a `reply(` site is an error edge only when what
it carries is an error, and the table above counts the token either way.
`config/api_routes.js` is the clearest case. It contributes exactly one
`reply(` site, which is why the row above reads 1 - and that site is
`return reply(true)`, an inline pre-handler answering with a boolean. It
carries no error, so it produces **no error-edge row**, and the file's
section below correctly holds none. A reconciliation that treated the site
count as evidence of edge coverage would have reported that file as covered
on the strength of a row that does not exist.

| | Sites (tokens) | Error-edge rows |
|---|---:|---:|
| `lib/controllers/*.js` | 0 `reply(` | 306 |
| `lib/util/helpers.js` | 6 `reply(` | 26 |
| `config/api_routes.js` | 0 `reply(` | 0 |

The counts were **reported, not asserted** because the analysed tree was not detected as the baseline: the legacy-handler fingerprint is 2 against a floor of 100, its HEAD is not the baseline commit, and the token counts already differ.
A differing count here is the conversion, not a scanner fault: the legacy
idiom is what these tokens measure and removing it is the work. The
scanner's own correctness is guaranteed independently, by 55 self-tests that
run on every invocation and include the regex-literal-with-quotes hazard.

## 7. The inventory

Grouped by file, then by line. Re-running produces a reviewable diff, not a
reshuffle.

### 7.1 `config/api_routes.js` - 0 rows

No error edge detected in this file. The scanner counted 0 `reply(` site(s), 0 `.catch(` site(s) and 0 `err`-parameter function(s) here, and
classified every one of them as carrying a non-error value: the file was
analysed, not skipped.

### 7.2 `lib/controllers/admin.js` - 20 rows

- [x] `lib/controllers/admin.js:69-72` `id=admin.index.handler.1` - **swallows silently** - none - .catch() handler - absorbs
      Carrier `admin.index` (route handler) - Routes `GET /admin`, `GET /admin/{adminPage*}` - Source `.catch(function(err) {`
      Target: The error is absorbed: no response, no rethrow, no log. Funnel: none. Preserve the swallow exactly - R-d prohibits repairing it, and a caller that today proceeds on a failed operation must keep proceeding. Side effects: none at all - no log, no response, no rethrow, no flash. The error value is discarded where it is received, and R-d requires that it keep being discarded. Timing: deferred. The handler is registered synchronously while the chain at line 43 is built, and runs only when that chain rejects - a microtask at the earliest, and after the handler body has already returned. The chain is assigned to `promise` at that statement rather than returned, so whether anything waits for the rejection is decided where `promise` is consumed - preserve that consumption point, and do not move the settlement to this one.

The status the line READS as - 500 - is therefore not the status served: the handler catch-all logs err.stack at error level and returns Boom.badImplementation(err.message), so the client is answered **500** with Boom's generic 5xx body. Preserve the 500 and the log. R-d requires this: it is a defect, it is baseline behaviour, and repairing the binding would change an observable status. Side effects: Layer 1 logs `err.stack` at error level, or `String(err)` when there is no stack. Nothing else is written. Timing: synchronous, on the handler body's own stack, before it returns.

- [ ] `lib/controllers/users.js:2074` `id=users.downloadExport.response.1` - **calls request.fail locally** - Layer 2 - request.fail({ error: err.message }) with no return
      Carrier `users.downloadExport` (route handler) - Routes `GET /api/exports/{exportId}/download` - Source `return resolve(request.fail({ error: err.message }));`
      Target: Layer 2. request.fail logs its argument at info level, then selects one of three responses: with a negotiated `html` type and a configured `fail.redirect` it flashes `failure`, interpolates `fail.redirect` IN PLACE on the parse-time route object - the cross-request leak - flashes `payload` and `query`, and redirects; with `html`, a configured `fail.html` and no `.json` extension on the path it renders that view through addUserContext; otherwise it returns h.response(json) with `json.flash` attached. Preserve the branch selection, both flash writes, the status and the body. The response is not a Boom, so the onPreResponse extension takes its `else if (response.header)` branch and applies Cache-Control, Pragma and Expires. The emulation is gone from this tree, so the returned value IS the response: this path must return it exactly once, because a path that returns nothing is converted by hapi into Boom.badImplementation - a 500 in place of whatever this edge answers - and a returned-but-unawaited chain answers with a pending promise instead of its value. This call site has no `return`, which is immaterial under the shim and decisive after conversion. Side effects: request.fail logs its argument at info level when one is present; on the `html` + `fail.redirect` branch it writes the `failure` flash (only when a payload was passed), mutates `fail.redirect` IN PLACE on the object captured at parse time, and writes the `payload` and `query` flashes; on the other two branches it calls `request.yar.flash()` with no arguments, which READS AND CLEARS the accumulated flash into the response body. Preserve exactly those writes - no more, and none dropped. The in-place mutation is the cross-request leak catalogued in docs/preserved-quirks.md section 3 and is required, not repaired. Timing: inside the callback `Export.findById` invokes, off the handler's own stack, so it runs after the handler body has already returned.
      Closure: pairing ambiguous - not compared
      Driven: route-level only, by `route.get.api-exports-exportId-download.json`. Those scenarios drive the route, which is not the same as reaching this failure branch: only an `error-edge.*` scenario naming `id=users.downloadExport.response.1` proves the branch was exercised.

Side effects: on the resolving path the pre-handler's assigned value becomes the value passed here, so `request.pre.<assign>` holds it for every later stage that reads it; on the rejecting path nothing is assigned. ## 8. Closure against the target tree

Each row above is joined to the row measuring the same edge on the other
tree and closed only when the two agree on EVERY dimension R-e names:

- funnel
- status served
- whether a response is produced
- surface
- payload or redirect shape
- side effects
- log calls
- settlement timing

Comparing the status alone is not closure, and it was what an earlier
edition of this document compared: 16 rows closed on a matching status
while their payload, their side effects or their settlement had moved, and
R-e exists to catch exactly those.

The MECHANISM is deliberately not compared - a `reply(Boom.notFound())` and
a `return Boom.notFound()` are one outcome by two means, and changing the
means IS the migration. Keeping the mechanism out took three calibrations,
each forced by a measurement rather than assumed:

- **Timing** compares whether the edge settles before the carrier returns,
  later with the carrier waiting, or later with nothing waiting. Naming the
  vehicle instead - a callback against an awaited chain - reported 54 rows
  as retimed for having been converted exactly as rule T-3 requires, and
  reading a registered `.catch` handler's declaration site as its
  settlement moved another 38 from deferred to synchronous.
- **Produced responses** compare their semantic KIND - a failure response, a
  success response, a redirect, a rendered view - and not the callee that
  produced them. `reply` was the shim's one universal producer, so its own
  kind is not knowable from the name; a row whose only producer was `reply`
  says so rather than claiming a kind.
- **A bare `.catch(reply)`** carries the rejection value itself, so its
  payload is an error value and not an unclassifiable one.

| Verdict | Rows | What it means |
|---|---:|---|
| closed | 245 | baseline and target agree on every dimension above |
| closed by approved deviation | 0 | the outcome changed, and this exact edge and this exact change are on the approved list |
| **open - outcome changed** | 27 | R-e requires these to match and they do not; each row names the dimensions |
| **open - no target row** | 41 | the baseline edge could not be located in the target |
| **open - new in the target** | 28 | no baseline fact to preserve, so nothing is verified |
| **open - pairing ambiguous** | 23 | the two trees order the same ids differently, so no pairing in that carrier can be established |
| not compared - proven unreachable | 9 | nothing in the corpus reaches the edge, so there is no outcome on either tree to compare |

| Reconciliation | Rows |
|---|---:|
| baseline rows, all accounted for | 342 of 342 |
| target rows, all accounted for | 332 of 332 |
| paired by exact identity | 223 |
| paired by source order within the carrier | 78 |
| of those, where an identically-named target row exists elsewhere | 24 |

Identity is authoritative and source order fills its gaps, in that order.
An identity is `<file>.<carrier>.<class>.<ordinal>`, so it denotes the same
edge on both trees only while that carrier's population of that CLASS is
unchanged; where it changed, the ordinals renumbered and the id match is an
artefact. Measured on `course.deleteCourse`, whose first baseline edge
became the handler-class edge and whose second kept its shape, taking the
id match would pair line 151 with line 192 and report both rows changed -
two false failures from one crossing. So an id match anchors the alignment
only where the class population is stable, and the 24 rows where it is
not say so on their own row rather than having the choice hidden. Where the
anchors that ARE trusted appear in a different order on the two trees,
nothing about that carrier can be established from position and every pair
in it is reported ambiguous instead of given a verdict.

### The approved-deviation list

**Empty, by measurement.** AAP 0.7 records exactly one approved behaviour
deviation on this surface - the never-settling image branch of the file
download - and its `reply(stream)` calls carry a stream rather than an
error, so no error edge exists for it and there is nothing to exempt here.
An entry would have to name the exact row id AND the exact from/to
outcome; a difference that merely claimed to be approved would still be
reported open, because approving a deviation approves one reviewed change
and not any change under the same name.

The gate is reported only on this run. Pass `--closure-gate` to exit non-zero while any row is open.

## 9. Driven coverage

Joined against `parity/corpus.json`: 383 scenarios, of which 9 are `error-edge.*` across the groups `error-edge.error-page-headers`, `error-edge.log-and-continue`, `error-edge.not-found`.

| Coverage | Rows | What it proves |
|---|---:|---|
| edge-level | 0 | a scenario names this row's id, so the branch itself was reached |
| route-level only | 286 | a scenario drives the row's route, which is not the same as reaching its failure branch |
| **not driven** | 46 | no scenario names the row or its routes |

**Route-level is not coverage of an error edge.** One minimal request per
route exercises success paths; an error edge needs a request that reaches
its own branch. The distinction is the reason this table has three rows
rather than two, and the reason a route-level row is reported as
unverified above.

### Changed edges with no scenario of their own

**128** of the open rows in section 8 have no `error-edge.*` scenario naming their id, so
their target outcome is a reading of the code rather than a measured
response. Each one needs a scenario in `test/parity/capture.js` whose
`coversEdges` array carries the id - `covers` is route-keys only and
capture.js rejects anything else there:

## 11. Drivability

`test/parity/capture.js`'s failure-path scenarios are derived from these rows -
by hand, in its `error-edge.*` groups, not by parsing this file - and
`test/parity/replay.js` cites this checklist as what supplies the failure paths
the success sweep cannot reach. A row nothing can drive is not a testable
edge, so every row names the MECHANISM that reaches it - and where nothing
does, says so with the search that proves it.

Driveability is a FIELD on each row - `driveVia` - resolved from
reachability rather than inferred from the row carrying metadata. Two
earlier versions of this check were dead in the same way, and the second is
worth stating because it looked like a fix: it tested
`carrierMember && surface !== module`, which every non-module row satisfies
by construction, so a function that nothing anywhere calls came back
drivable. Each value below therefore names something the analysis RESOLVED:
a route declaration, a `server.method` registration, a lifecycle export, a
traced caller, module load - or a corpus-wide search returning nothing but
the declaration itself, which is a proof of unreachability rather than an
absence of evidence. `unresolved` is fatal in the generator, and that
assertion is live; so is the check that this table accounts for every row.

| How the row is driven | Rows |
|---|---:|
| a bound route declaration | 282 |
| a lifecycle export bound by at least one route | 13 |
| a lifecycle export hapi can invoke, named by no route here | 6 |
| a `server.method` registration, through the string-form dispatcher | 6 |
| a traced caller - a call, or its value handed on | 20 |
| module load, which every require of the file runs | 0 |
| **proven unreachable** - the corpus mentions it only where it is declared | 5 |
| **nothing resolved** - fatal | 0 |

The **proven unreachable** rows are listed rather than counted. Each was
searched for across the analysis targets, both route modules, the route
parser and the bootstrap, and found only at its own declaration. Nothing
can drive them, so they carry no funnel claim, they are not ticked, and the
closure gate excludes them - an unreachable edge has no observable outcome,
so calling it closed would be asserting parity of nothing against nothing:

- `lib/controllers/auth.js:341-345` `id=auth.attempt.handler.1` - .catch() handler - absorbs. Corpus mentions outside its declaration: 0.
- `lib/controllers/users.js:419` `id=users.attempt.response.1` - throw denial. Corpus mentions outside its declaration: 0.
- `lib/controllers/users.js:463` `id=users.attempt.response.2` - throw coding. Corpus mentions outside its declaration: 0.
- `lib/controllers/users.js:483` `id=users.attempt.response.3` - throw new Error('Exceeded maxRedirects. Probably stuck in a red.... Corpus mentions outside its declaration: 0.
- `lib/util/helpers.js:60-63` `id=helpers.userByLogin.errparam.1` - error parameter of a callback to `User.findByLogin` - handed onward. Corpus mentions outside its declaration: 0.

No row is unresolved: every row either names a driver or carries the
search that proves it has none. That is the property that makes this
checklist convertible into failure-path scenarios - for the 327 rows that
have a driver - and it is what keeps the 5 that do not out of the gate
rather than silently inside it.

Route bindings are read from the `route :` declarations in `config/routes.js` and `config/api_routes.js`.
`config/routes.js` expands a subset of those per language at parse time, so the
registered surface is larger than the declarations named here; the expanded
route manifest belongs to `test/parity/manifest.js`. A declaration built by
concatenation is read through the concatenation and rendered with its
non-literal operands as `<name>`, so `GET /<lang>/{shortCode}` is a template
standing for one registered route per configured language - not a path with a
`lang` parameter.

A row whose carrier names no declaration is reached **through a traced
caller**, and tracing covers indirect dispatch as well as call syntax: a
function value passed as an argument, stored in an array or object, assigned
to a `pre` entry, re-exported, or registered with `server.method` is invoked
somewhere its own name is never followed by a `(`. Recognising only call
syntax reported such a function as having no caller, which understates its
reachability in exactly the direction that matters.

| Carrier surface | Rows |
|---|---:|
| route handler | 276 |
| pre-handler | 19 |
| server method | 6 |
